, and the fact that
\hat{f}_{\lambda},f_{\mathrm{aux}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}
.
The above equation is a sketch where formal derivations can be found at the beginning of
Appendix

D
, particularly Eq. (
74
) and Eq. (
75
).
Combining the above relation and the choice of
\gamma_{x}
in Eq. (
20
) concludes the proof of
4.1
.

Remark 4.7

(Extension to more anisotropy)
.

For simplicity of presentation, we focus on the case where anisotropic smoothness exists across
(X,O)
but we assume no anisotropy within
X
and
O
.
The KIV-O algorithm with adaptive length-scales and its associated learning rate can both be easily extended to the fully anisotropic setting, where
f_{\ast}\in B_{2,\infty}^{\bm{s}}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{O})
with
\bm{s}=[s_{1},\ldots,s_{d_{x}},s_{d_{x}+1},\ldots,s_{d_{x}+d_{o}}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}+d_{o}}
, a generalization of previous results from anisotropic nonparametric regression
(
Hang and Steinwart 2021
;
Suzuki and Nitanda 2021
;
Hoffman and Lepski 2002
)
to anisotropic NPIV-O. This is particularly relevant in applied work, where the observed covariates
O
are often of high dimensionality, because practitioners tend to adjust for as many observed covariates as possible to mitigate unobserved confounding. In such cases, our KIV-O algorithm with adaptive length-scales adapts to the intrinsic smoothness with respect to
O
. This mitigates the slow rates typically caused by a high ambient dimension when the intrinsic smoothness is high, as our learning rates avoid being limited by the worst-case smoothness across all dimensions.

4.3
Minimax lower bound for NPIV-O

In
4.2
, we prove a minimax lower bound for the NPIV-O problem. We call
admissible
a distribution
P_{ZXOY}
over
(Z,X,O,Y)
satisfying
2.2
,
4.1
,
4.4
and
4.5
, inducing a model of the form

\displaystyle Y=f_{\ast}(X,O)+\epsilon,\quad\E[\epsilon|Z,O]=0\;,

where
f_{\ast}
satisfies
2.3
and the conditional expectation operator
T:L^{2}(P_{XO})\to L^{2}(P_{ZO})
satisfies
4.3
. For an
admissible
distribution
P_{ZXOY}
, consider
\mathcal{D}=(\mathbf{z}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{o}_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{N},N\geq 1
sampled i.i.d from
P_{ZXOY}
and consider
\mathcal{D}_{1}=(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{i},\tilde{\mathbf{o}}_{i},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i})_{i=1}^{\tilde{n}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}=(\mathbf{z}_{i},\mathbf{o}_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
with
\tilde{n},n\leq N
.

Theorem 4.2

(Minimax lower bound)
.

There exists an
admissible
distribution
P_{ZXOY}
such that for all learning methods
(\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2})\mapsto\hat{f}_{(\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2})}
, for all
\tau>0
, and sufficiently large
n\geq 1
, the following minimax lower bound holds with
P^{n}
-probability at least
1-C_{1}\tau^{2}
and
P^{\tilde{n}}
-almost surely ,

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{f}_{(\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2})}-f_{\ast}\right\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}\geq C_{0}\tau^{2}n^{-\frac{\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}}{1+2(\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}+\eta_{1})+\frac{d_{o}}{s_{o}}\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}}}(\log n)^{-2s_{x}-d_{x}}.

(28)

We remind the reader here of our upper bound in Eq. (
22
): with
P^{n+\tilde{n}}
-probability at least
1-40e^{-\tau}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\hat{f}_{\lambda}\right]-f_{\ast}\right\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}\lesssim\tau n^{-\frac{\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}+\eta_{1}-\eta_{0}}{1+2(\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}+\eta_{1})+\frac{d_{o}}{s_{o}}(\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}+\eta_{1})}}\cdot(\log n)^{\frac{d_{x}+d_{o}+1+d_{x}\eta_{0}}{2}}.

There remains a gap between the upper and minimax lower bounds even if we take
\eta_{1}=\eta_{0}
and ignore the logarithmic terms. We dedicate
Section

5
to its discussion.

Remark 4.8

(Interpolation between NPIV and nonparametric regression)
.

Similar to the
4.4
of the upper learning rate,
our minimax lower learning rate also interpolates between the known optimal
L^{2}
-rates for NPIV without observed covariates (
d_{o}=0
) and anisotropic
non-parametric regression (
\eta_{1}=\eta_{0}=0
).

Remark 4.9

(Comparison to existing
L^{2}
-minimax lower bounds for NPIV regression)
.

The work
Chen and Reiss 2011
established minimax rate-optimality in
L^{2}
-norm for NPIV under an
approximation condition
and
link condition
. For a conveniently chosen compact operator
B
, the approximation condition characterizes the smoothness of the structural function by the optimal
L^{2}
-rate approximation by the eigenvectors of
B
, and the link condition relates the mapping properties of the conditional expectation operator
T
to the Hilbert scale generated by
B
. This framework subsumes an earlier minimax convergence rate result in
Hall and Horowitz 2005
, and was subsequently instantiated in
Chen and Christensen 2018
for a
B
constructed via a wavelet basis, and generalized to kernel instrumental variables in
Meunier et al. 2024a
with a Hilbert scale given by the covariance operator of the RKHS. A crucial limitation of this framework is the link condition can only hold if the singular values of the conditional expectation operator are decaying to zero. Thus the minimax framework given by the link condition is applicable only when
T
is compact, and is not suitable for our observed covariates setting.

We give an outline of our proof for
4.2
to facilitate a deeper
understanding of both the assumptions and the results. The full proof is in
Section

E.2
in the Supplementary. The primary strategy in deriving minimax lower bounds is to construct a family of distributions that are similar enough so that they are statistically indistinguishable, but for which the target function of interest is maximally separated.
This implies no estimator can have error uniformly smaller than this separation.

Following prior work in the literature
(
Chen and Reiss 2011
;
Meunier et al. 2024a
)
, we observe that NPIV-O in Eq. (
1
) is statistically more challenging than the reduced form non-parametric indirect regression with observed covariates (NPIR-O) with a
known
operator
T:L^{2}(P_{XO})\to L^{2}(P_{ZO})
. The NPIR-O model is defined below

\displaystyle Y=(Tf_{*})(Z,O)+\upsilon,

(29)

where
\upsilon
is a random variable such that
\mathbb{E}[\upsilon\mid Z,O]=0
, and it satisfies
4.5
, and where
T
satisfies
4.1
,
4.3
and the associated conditional distribution
P_{X\mid Z,O}
satisfies
2.2
. We refer the reader to
Section

E.1
in the Supplementary Material for a detailed definition of the NPIR-O model class. We formally prove in
E.1
in the Supplementary that it suffices to construct a minimax lower bound for the NPIR-O model.
To this end, we adapt Theorem 20 of
Fischer and Steinwart 2020
, which is itself an adaptation of Proposition 2.3 of
Tsybakov 2008
. Recall that the
Kullback-Leibler divergence
of two probability measures
P_{1},P_{2}
on some measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
is given by
\mathrm{KL}(P_{1},P_{2}):=\int_{\Omega}\log(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{1}}{\mathrm{d}P_{2}})\;\mathrm{d}P_{1}

if
P_{1}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P_{2}
, and
+\infty
otherwise.

To apply Theorem 20 of
Fischer and Steinwart 2020
on the measurable space
\Omega=(\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})^{n}
, we construct a family of probability measures
P_{0},P_{1},\dots,P_{M}
over
(\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R})
that share the same marginal distribution over
(Z,O)
but different conditional distributions
P_{Y\mid Z,O}
. Our strategy follows the construction in
Chen and Reiss 2011
and can be explained as follows. We fix a marginal probability measure
P_{ZO}
over
\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}
. We then propose a family of conditional probability measures
P_{i;Y\mid Z,O}
indexed by
f_{i}\in\mathfrak{F}
for
0\leq i\leq M
. Since
f_{i}
’s are functions on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{O}
, we fix a marginal probability measure
P_{X}
on
\mathcal{X}
under the constraint that
X
is independent of
O
. Then, we define a smooth copula to parametrize the dependence between
P_{X}
and
P_{Z}
, which fully specifies
P_{X\mid Z,O}
. This induces a fixed conditional expectation operator
T:L^{2}(P_{XO})\to L^{2}(P_{ZO})
. We then specify
P_{i;Y\mid Z,O}
for
0\leq i\leq M
via the following equation:

\displaystyle Y\mid Z=\mathbf{z},O=\mathbf{o}\sim\mathcal{N}((Tf_{i})(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o}),\sigma^{2}),

for a fixed
\sigma>0
, and for any
(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\in\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}
.
P_{i}
denotes the probability measure on
\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
by coupling
P_{i;Y\mid Z,O}
and
P_{ZO}
. In the above construction, we require
P_{X\mid Z,O}
to satisfy
2.2
, and all
f_{i}
’s to satisfy
2.3
, namely
f_{i}\in\mathfrak{S}
.

Concretely, we fix
P_{ZO}
to be the Lebesgue measure over
\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}
. Without loss of generality, we take
\mathcal{X}=[-0.5,0.5]^{d_{x}}
rather than
[0,1]^{d_{x}}
. By working with a symmetric domain, we exploit the fact that even functions have real-valued Fourier transforms, which simplify our subsequent calculations. We fix
P_{X}
via the density function

\textstyle p_{X}(\mathbf{x})\propto\prod_{i=1}^{d_{x}}g_{i}(x_{i}),\quad g_{i}(x_{i}):=\exp\left(-\frac{2}{1-4x_{i}^{2}}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{x_{i}\in[-0.5,0.5]},

(30)

where each
g_{i}
is a smooth, compactly supported
bump function
. We fix an arbitrary smooth copula
(
Nelsen 2006
)
, such that
P_{X\mid Z,O}
satisfies
2.2
and
T
satisfies
4.3
with parameter
\eta_{1}>0
.

The main technical challenge of the minimax lower bound is the construction of the function class
\mathfrak{F}
whose each element
f_{i}
induces a conditional distribution
P_{X\mid Z,O}
.
Our goal is to design
\mathfrak{F}
so that it satisfies three desirable properties: 1) we want
\mathfrak{F}\in B_{2,\infty}^{s_{x},s_{o}}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}+d_{o}})
as per
2.3
.
2) we want the size of
\mathfrak{F}
to be large to enable the application of
Fischer and Steinwart 2020
.
3) we want all elements of
\mathfrak{F}
to exhibit high partial Fourier frequency such that after applying
T
, the resulting functions become difficult to distinguish.
This increases the intrinsic difficulty of the problem and yields a tighter lower bound.
The construction of such a function class satisfying the first two properties is standard and can be achieved using anisotropic B-splines
(
Ibragimov and Khas’ minskii 1984
;
Suzuki and Nitanda 2021
;
Schmidt-Hieber 2020
)
.
However, the third property poses a challenge: the Fourier transform of B-splines has full support.
Consequently, conventional B-splines cannot enforce the desired partial high-frequency restriction. To address this limitation, we convolve the B-splines with a high frequency bandpass filter in the
X
-direction.
Since convolution acts as a smoothing operator, the function class constructed with such modified B-splines remains in the Besov space.

To start with, we introduce the definition of anisotropic B-splines
(
Leisner 2003
)
.

Definition 4

(Anisotropic B-spline
(
Leisner 2003
)
)
.

The cardinal B-spline of order
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{N}
is defined as the repeated
\mathfrak{m}
times convolution
\iota_{\mathfrak{m}}=\iota_{0}\ast\dots\ast\iota_{0}
where
\iota_{0}
is the indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{[0,1]}
. Let
d
denote the ambient dimension, and let
\bm{s}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{d})
denote a smoothness vector. Define the notation
\underline{s}=\min(s_{1},\dots,s_{d})
and
s_{i}^{\prime}:=\frac{\underline{s}}{s_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq d
. The (anisotropic) B-spline of order
\mathfrak{m}
with resolution
\mathfrak{K}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and location vector
{\bm{\ell}}\in\prod_{i=1}^{d}\{-\mathfrak{m},-\mathfrak{m}+1,\dots,2^{\left\lfloor\mathfrak{K}s_{i}^{\prime}\right\rfloor}\}
is defined as
M_{\mathfrak{K},{\bm{\ell}}}(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\iota_{\mathfrak{m}}(2^{\lfloor\mathfrak{K}s_{i}^{\prime}\rfloor}x_{i}-\ell_{i})
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}>s_{x}\vee s_{o}
be a non-negative even integer.
The resolution
\mathfrak{K}=\mathfrak{K}(n)
is defined later in Eq. (
131
), such that
\mathfrak{K}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
The B-spline basis in the
O
-direction follows the standard construction. Define the set of location vectors
\mathcal{L}_{O}
and the associated B-splines

\textstyle\mathcal{L}_{O}:=\left\{2\mathfrak{m}\mathbb{N}\cap\left\{0,\dots,2^{\left\lfloor\mathfrak{K}\frac{\underline{s}}{s_{o}}\right\rfloor}\right\}\right\}^{d_{o}},\quad M_{\mathfrak{K},{\bm{\ell}}_{o}}(\mathbf{o})=\prod_{j=1}^{d_{o}}\iota_{\mathfrak{m}}\left(2^{\left\lfloor\frac{\mathfrak{K}\underline{s}}{s_{o}}\right\rfloor}o_{j}-\ell_{o,j}\right).

(31)

Note that here we enforce all location vectors to be a multiple of
2\mathfrak{m}
such that the
\{M_{\mathfrak{K},{\bm{\ell}}_{o}}\}_{{\bm{\ell}}_{o}\in\mathcal{L}_{O}}
have disjoint supports, which will simplify the calculations later on.
In contrast, the basis functions in the
X
-direction are constructed by convolving a standard B-spline
M_{0,-\frac{\mathfrak{m}}{2}}
on
X
with the inverse Fourier transform of the indicator function
\mathbbm{1}_{{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}
, for
{\bm{\ell}}_{x}\in\mathcal{L}_{X}
:

\textstyle\mathcal{L}_{X}

\textstyle:=\left\{0,1,\dots,\left\lfloor\frac{0.8\pi}{\zeta}2^{\mathfrak{K}\frac{\underline{s}}{s_{x}}}\right\rfloor\right\}^{d_{x}},\quad\Omega_{\mathfrak{K}{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}(\mathbf{x}):=\left(M_{0,-\frac{\mathfrak{m}}{2}}\ast\mathcal{F}^{-1}[\mathbbm{1}_{{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}]\right)\left(2^{\frac{\mathfrak{K}\underline{s}}{s_{x}}}\mathbf{x}\right).

(32)

where
\mathbbm{1}_{{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}
is the indicator function over the following hyper-rectangle:

\displaystyle I_{{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}:=\bigtimes_{j=1}^{d_{x}}\left[1.1\pi+\zeta\ell_{x,j}2^{-\mathfrak{K}\frac{\underline{s}}{s_{x}}},1.1\pi+(\zeta\ell_{x,j}+1)2^{-\mathfrak{K}\frac{\underline{s}}{s_{x}}}\right].

(33)

\zeta>0
is a width hyperparameter that determines the spacing of different hyper-rectangles; its value will be specified later.
For sufficiently large
n\geq 1
, since
\ell_{x,j}\leq\frac{0.8\pi}{\zeta}2^{\frac{\mathfrak{K}\underline{s}}{s_{x}}}
, we know that
1.1\pi+(\zeta\ell_{x,j}+1)2^{-\mathfrak{K}\frac{\underline{s}}{s_{x}}}\leq 1.9\pi+2^{-\mathfrak{K}\frac{\underline{s}}{s_{x}}}\leq 1.95\pi
. Thus we have
I_{{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}\subseteq[1.1\pi,1.95\pi]^{d_{x}}
.

The main consequence of this construction is revealed via the convolution theorem
(
Rudin 1987
, Theorem 9.2)
, which gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[\Omega_{\mathfrak{K}{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}](\bm{\omega}_{x})=\mathcal{F}[M_{\mathfrak{K},-\frac{\mathfrak{m}}{2}}](\bm{\omega}_{x})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}(2^{-\frac{\mathfrak{K}\underline{s}}{s_{x}}}\bm{\omega}_{x}).

(34)

As a result, we have that
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{F}[\Omega_{\mathfrak{K}{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}])=2^{\frac{\mathfrak{K}\underline{s}}{s_{x}}}\cdot I_{{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}\subseteq[\pi 2^{\frac{\mathfrak{K}\underline{s}}{s_{x}}},2\pi 2^{\frac{\mathfrak{K}\underline{s}}{s_{x}}}]^{d_{x}}
. Since
\mathfrak{K}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, the construction guarantees that
\Omega_{\mathfrak{K}{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}
only has high-frequency spectrum, thereby fulfilling the third desirable property for
\mathfrak{F}
.

Figure 2
:

This figure illustrates the partial Fourier transform of
f_{\mathbf{v}}
in Eq. (
35
), where we take
d_{x}=1
,
\mathfrak{K}=2
,
\mathfrak{m}=4
,
s_{x}=\underline{s}\leq s_{o}
.
The dashed blue line represents the Fourier transform of the B-spline
M_{\mathfrak{K},-2}
.
The red line shows the result of applying frequency masks to this B-spline:
\sum_{{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}\beta_{\mathbf{v}({\bm{\ell}}_{x},{\bm{\ell}}_{o})}\mathcal{F}[M_{\mathfrak{K},-2}](\omega_{x})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}(2^{-\mathfrak{K}}\omega_{x})
. The Fourier transform of
f_{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\mathbf{o})
is equal to this result up to scaling factors dependent only on
\mathbf{o}
.

Define the basis
\Omega_{\mathfrak{K}({\bm{\ell}}_{x},{\bm{\ell}}_{o})}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{o}):=\Omega_{\mathfrak{K}{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot M_{\mathfrak{K},{\bm{\ell}}_{o}}(\mathbf{o})
and the set of all location vectors
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{X}\times\mathcal{L}_{O}
.
We have
|\mathcal{L}|\asymp\zeta^{-d_{x}}2^{\mathfrak{K}\underline{s}(\frac{d_{x}}{s_{x}}+\frac{d_{o}}{s_{o}})}
.
We construct a function class
\mathfrak{F}
as follows:

\textstyle\mathfrak{F}:=\left\{f_{\mathbf{v}}:f_{\mathbf{v}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{o})=2^{-\mathfrak{K}\underline{s}\left(1-\frac{d_{x}}{2s_{x}}\right)}\sum_{({\bm{\ell}}_{x},{\bm{\ell}}_{o})\in\mathcal{L}}\beta_{\mathbf{v}({\bm{\ell}}_{x},{\bm{\ell}}_{o})}\Omega_{\mathfrak{K}({\bm{\ell}}_{x},{\bm{\ell}}_{o})}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{o})\,\middle|\,\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{L}|}\right\},

(35)

where
\beta_{\mathbf{v}({\bm{\ell}}_{x},{\bm{\ell}}_{o})}\in\{0,1\}
is the value assigned by
\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{L}|}
to the location vector
({\bm{\ell}}_{x},{\bm{\ell}}_{o})\in\mathcal{L}
.
Specifically, for each
f_{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathfrak{F}
and for a fixed
\mathbf{o}\in\mathcal{O}
, we can see that
f_{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\mathbf{o})
is constructed by applying a sum of
frequency masks
indexed by
{\bm{\ell}}_{x}\in\mathcal{L}_{X}
to the original B-spline
M_{\mathfrak{K},-\frac{\mathfrak{m}}{2}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[f_{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot,\mathbf{o})](\bm{\omega}_{x})=\mathcal{F}\left[M_{\mathfrak{K},-\frac{\mathfrak{m}}{2}}\right](\bm{\omega}_{x})\cdot\Big(\sum_{{\bm{\ell}}_{o}\in\mathcal{L}_{O}}\underbrace{\sum_{{\bm{\ell}}_{x}\in\mathcal{L}_{X}}\beta_{\mathbf{v}({\bm{\ell}}_{x},{\bm{\ell}}_{o})}\mathbbm{1}_{{\bm{\ell}}_{x}}\left(2^{-\frac{\mathfrak{K}\underline{s}}{s_{x}}}\bm{\omega}_{x}\right)}_{\text{frequency masks}}M_{\mathfrak{K},{\bm{\ell}}_{o}}(\mathbf{o})\Big).

Figure

2
is provided to illustrate this effect of frequency masking.

We prove in Eq. (
108
) in the Supplementary that
\mathfrak{F}\subseteq\mathbb{U}(B^{s_{x},s_{o}}_{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}+d_{o}}))\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}+d_{o}})\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}+d_{o}})\cap C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}+d_{o}})\subseteq\mathfrak{S}
, satisfying
2.3
.
For each
f_{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathfrak{F}
, we construct the conditional distribution
P_{\mathbf{v}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o}):=\mathcal{N}((Tf_{\mathbf{v}})(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o}),\sigma^{2})
as the normal distribution on
\mathbb{R}
with mean
(Tf_{\mathbf{v}})(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})
and variance
\sigma^{2}
, such that
P_{\mathbf{v}}
satisfies
4.5
. Together with the marginal distribution
P_{ZO}
over
\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}
, we have constructed a family of distributions
\{P_{\mathbf{v}},\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{L}|}\}
over
\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}
with each
P_{\mathbf{v}}
associated to a
f_{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathfrak{F}
.
Next, by the Gilbert-Varshamov Bound
(
Tsybakov 2008
, Lemma 2.9)
, we prove in Eq. (
E.2
) and Eq. (
127
) in the Supplementary that there exists a subset
V_{\mathfrak{K}}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{L}|}
with

|V_{\mathfrak{K}}|\geq 2^{\frac{\mathcal{L}}{8}}
such that for any
f_{\mathbf{v}},f_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{pruned}}=\{f_{\mathbf{v}}\mid\mathbf{v}\in V_{\mathfrak{K}}\}
, there is

\displaystyle\|f_{\mathbf{v}}-f_{\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}\geq 2^{-2\mathfrak{K}\underline{s}}\zeta^{-d_{x}},\quad\mathrm{KL}\left(P_{\bm{v}}^{\otimes n},P_{\bm{0}}^{\otimes n}\right)\leq n2^{-2\mathfrak{K}\underline{s}(\frac{d_{x}}{s_{x}}\eta_{1}+1)}.

Therefore, we have established that the function class
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{pruned}}
satisfies all the three properties as desired, namely
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{pruned}}\subset\mathfrak{G}
, functions
f_{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathrm{pruned}}
are well-separated in
L^{2}(P_{XO})
yet statistically indistinguishable.
Finally, we take
\zeta=(\log n)^{2}
, we apply
Fischer and Steinwart 2020
and the general reduction scheme in
Tsybakov 2008
to obtain the desired minimax lower bound.

5
On the gap between the upper bound and minimax lower bound

As explained in
Section

4.3
, in the general setting where
\frac{d_{x}}{s_{x}}>0
and
\frac{d_{o}}{s_{o}}>0
, a gap arises between the upper bound given in
4.1
and the minimax lower bound given in
4.2
. By setting
\eta_{0}=\eta_{1}=\eta
in
4.3
and
4.2
, which gives a precise characterization of the
partial
smoothing effect of
T
, we obtain the following upper and lower bounds (ignoring the logarithmic terms for simplicity):

\displaystyle\text{Lower Bound:}\quad n^{-\frac{\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}}{1+2(\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}+\eta)+\frac{d_{o}}{s_{o}}\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}}},\quad\quad\text{Upper Bound:}\quad n^{-\frac{\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}}{1+2(\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}+\eta)+\frac{d_{o}}{s_{o}}\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}+\frac{d_{o}}{s_{o}}\eta}}

Note that the denominator in the exponent of the upper bound contains an extra
\frac{d_{o}}{s_{o}}\eta
term, which vanishes when
T
has no
partial
smoothing effect (
\eta=0
) or when the ratio
\frac{d_{o}}{s_{o}}
is zero.

The gap between the upper bound and the minimax lower bound arises due to the existence of observed covariates
O
in the analysis of the approximation error term in the upper bound,

\displaystyle\|[f_{\lambda}]-f_{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})},

where
f_{\lambda}
is defined in Eq. (
25
). To provide an in-depth discussion, we begin with a warm-up in
Section

5.1
by considering the standard KIV setting without observed covariates, where our upper and minimax lower bounds match. We then move on to KIV-O in
Section

5.2
and demonstrate how the presence of observed covariates
O
causes a gap to emerge.

5.1
Analysis of the approximation error in KIV

In NPIV, the conditional expectation operator is
T:L^{2}(P_{X})\to L^{2}(P_{Z})
,
f\mapsto\E[f(X)\mid Z]
.
Hence
f_{\lambda}
defined in Eq. (
25
) reduces to the following

\textstyle f_{\lambda}:=\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{H}_{X,\gamma_{x}}}\lambda\|f\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{X,\gamma_{x}}}+\|T([f]-f_{\ast})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{Z})}.

We first employ the fact that
f_{\lambda}
is the minimizer, hence for some
f_{\mathrm{aux}}\in\mathcal{H}_{X,\gamma_{x}}
,

\displaystyle\|T([f_{\lambda}]-f_{\ast})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{Z})}\leq\lambda\|f_{\mathrm{aux}}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{X,\gamma_{x}}}+\|T([f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-f_{\ast})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{Z})}.

Here,
f_{\mathrm{aux}}
is defined as

f_{\mathrm{aux}}:=f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}+K_{\gamma_{x}}\ast f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}
, where
K_{\gamma_{x}}
is defined, following
Hang and Steinwart 2021
;
Eberts and Steinwart 2013
, as

\textstyle K_{\gamma_{x}}(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\binom{r}{j}(-1)^{1-j}\frac{1}{(j\gamma_{x})^{d}}\left(\frac{2}{\pi}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}\exp\left(-2\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{x_{i}^{2}}{(j\gamma_{x})^{2}}\right),

(36)

and
f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}},f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}
are defined such that
f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}
(resp.
f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}
) corresponds to the low-frequency (resp. high-frequency) component of
f_{\ast}=f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}+f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}](\bm{\omega}_{x})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}[f_{\ast}](\bm{\omega}_{x})\cdot\mathbbm{1}[\bm{\omega}_{x}:\|\bm{\omega}_{x}\|_{2}\leq\gamma_{x}^{-1}]

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}](\bm{\omega}_{x})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}[f_{\ast}](\bm{\omega}_{x})\cdot\mathbbm{1}[\bm{\omega}_{x}:\|\bm{\omega}_{x}\|_{2}\geq\gamma_{x}^{-1}].

To see why
f_{\mathrm{aux}}\in\mathcal{H}_{X,\gamma_{x}}
, note that

\|f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{X,\gamma_{x}}}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}}}|\mathcal{F}[f_{\ast}](\bm{\omega}_{x})|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\bm{\omega}_{x}=\|f_{\ast}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})}<\infty
and
K_{\gamma_{x}}\ast f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}\in\mathcal{H}_{X,\gamma_{x}}
proved by
Eberts and Steinwart 2013
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\|T([f_{\lambda}]-f_{\ast})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{Z})}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\|f_{\mathrm{aux}}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{X,\gamma_{x}}}+\|T(f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}-K_{\gamma_{x}}\ast f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{Z})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{\leq}}\lambda\|f_{\mathrm{aux}}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{X,\gamma_{x}}}+\gamma_{x}^{2d_{x}\eta}\|f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}-K_{\gamma_{x}}\ast f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}

(37)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast\ast)}}{{\lesssim}}\lambda\gamma_{x}^{-d_{x}}+\gamma_{x}^{2d_{x}\eta+2s_{x}}.

where
(\ast)
follows by
4.3
, and
(\ast\ast)
follows by the same derivations in
Eberts and Steinwart 2013
.
Eq. (
37
) is the key step which would require significant modifications in the setting of NPIV-O due to the existence of observed covariates. Finally, we have

\displaystyle\quad\|[f_{\lambda}]-f_{\ast}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}

\displaystyle\leq\|[f_{\lambda}]-[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}+\|[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-f_{\ast}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\gamma_{x}^{-2d_{x}\eta}\|T[f_{\lambda}]-T[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{Z})}+\|[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-f_{\ast}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{x}^{-2d_{x}\eta}\left(\|T([f_{\lambda}]-f_{\ast})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{Z})}+\|T([f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-f_{\ast})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{Z})}\right)+\|[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-f_{\ast}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\gamma_{x}^{-2d_{x}\eta}\left(\|T([f_{\lambda}]-f_{\ast})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{Z})}+\gamma_{x}^{2d_{x}\eta}\|[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-f_{\ast}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}\right)+\|[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-f_{\ast}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{x}^{-2d_{x}\eta}\left(\lambda\gamma_{x}^{-d_{x}}+\gamma_{x}^{2d_{x}\eta+2s_{x}}\right)+2\|[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-f_{\ast}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\lesssim}}n^{-\frac{2s_{x}}{d_{x}+2s_{x}+2\eta d_{x}}}.

In the above derivations,
(a)
follows by an application of
4.2
and
D.9
since
\|f_{\lambda}\|^{2}\leq\lambda^{-1}\|Tf_{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(P_{Z})}^{2}\lesssim n
by the optimality of
f_{\lambda}
and
\|f_{\text{aux}}\|\leq n
by the same derivations as in Eq. (
67
) in the Supplement. The second term of Eq. (
96
) in the Supplement is subsumed by the following choice of
\gamma_{x}
.
Furthermore,
(b)
follows from
4.3
, and
(c)
follows from using Eq. (
84
) in the Supplement and choosing
\lambda=n^{-1}
,
\gamma_{x}=n^{-\frac{1}{d_{x}+2s_{x}+2\eta d_{x}}}
.
The upper bound on the approximation error above is
optimal
in the sense that it matches the minimax lower bound of NPIV with Besov targets (see e.g
(
Chen and Christensen 2018
)
and our Theorem 4.2 with
d_{o}=0
).

5.2
Analysis of the approximation error in KIV-O

We now proceed to our NPIV-O setting, and demonstrate how the existence of observed covariates
O
fundamentally changes the problem. As above, to upper bound the approximation error
\|[f_{\lambda}]-f_{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}
, we first need to upper bound the projected approximation error
\|Tf_{\lambda}-Tf_{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}
. To this end, we employ the fact that
f_{\lambda}
is the minimizer in Eq. (
25
), hence for some
f_{\mathrm{aux}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}
,

\displaystyle\|T([f_{\lambda}]-f_{\ast})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}\leq\lambda\|f_{\mathrm{aux}}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}+\|T([f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-f_{\ast})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}.

We construct such an auxiliary function
f_{\mathrm{aux}}
. For any
\mathbf{o}\in\mathcal{O}
, we construct
f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}(\cdot,\mathbf{o})
and
f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}(\cdot,\mathbf{o})
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}(\cdot,\mathbf{o})](\bm{\omega}_{x})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}[f_{\ast}(\cdot,\mathbf{o})](\bm{\omega}_{x})\cdot\mathbbm{1}[\bm{\omega}_{x}:\|\bm{\omega}_{x}\|_{2}\leq\gamma_{x}^{-1}]

(38)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}(\cdot,\mathbf{o})](\bm{\omega}_{x})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}[f_{\ast}(\cdot,\mathbf{o})](\bm{\omega}_{x})\cdot\mathbbm{1}[\bm{\omega}_{x}:\|\bm{\omega}_{x}\|_{2}\geq\gamma_{x}^{-1}].

(39)

Note that
f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}=f_{\ast}-f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}
. Despite
f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}(\cdot,\mathbf{o})\in\mathcal{H}_{X,\gamma_{x}}
for any
\mathbf{o}\in\mathcal{O}
, a crucial difference with NPIV is that,
f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}\notin\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}
since
f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}
is only constructed by a cut-off with respect to the partial Fourier spectrum of on
X
. Hence, to construct
f_{\mathrm{aux}}
we convolve
f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}
again with
K_{\gamma_{o}}
:

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{aux}}=f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}\ast K_{\gamma_{o}}+f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}\ast K_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}.

(40)

Here,
\;K_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{o}):=K_{\gamma_{x}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot K_{\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o})
. Proceeding from the above, we have

\displaystyle\quad\|T([f_{\lambda}]-f_{\ast})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}\leq\lambda\|f_{\mathrm{aux}}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\|T(f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}-f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}\ast K_{\gamma_{o}})\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}^{2}+\|T(f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}-f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}\ast K_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}})\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}^{2}

(41)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\|f_{\mathrm{aux}}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}+\|f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}-f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}\ast K_{\gamma_{o}}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\gamma_{x}^{2d_{x}\eta}\|f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}-f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}\ast K_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}^{2}.

The last inequality follows by Jensen’s inequality for the second term and
4.3
for the third term.
We would like to highlight here that the second term in Eq. (
41
) is absent in Eq. (
37
) for the NPIV setting when there are no observed covariates
O
. Unlike the high frequency term in Eq. (
41
), we cannot employ the Fourier measure of
partial
contractivity in
4.3
because
f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}-f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}\ast K_{\gamma_{o}}
only contains low frequency spectrum on
X
by construction.
From the above derivations, we can also deduce

\displaystyle\|T([f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-f_{\ast})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}-f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}\ast K_{\gamma_{o}}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\gamma_{x}^{2d_{x}\eta}\|f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}-f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}\ast K_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}^{2}.

To upper bound the approximation error
\|[f_{\lambda}]-f_{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}
, we notice that

\displaystyle\quad\|[f_{\lambda}]-f_{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|[f_{\lambda}]-[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}+\|[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-f_{\ast}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{x}^{-2d_{x}\eta}\|T([f_{\lambda}]-[f_{\mathrm{aux}}])\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}^{2}+\|[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-f_{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{x}^{-2d_{x}\eta}\|T([f_{\lambda}]-f_{\ast})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}+\gamma_{x}^{-2d_{x}\eta}\|T[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-Tf_{\ast}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}+\|[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-f_{\ast}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{x}^{-2d_{x}\eta}\Big(\lambda\|f_{\mathrm{aux}}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}+\|f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}-f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}\ast K_{\gamma_{o}}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\gamma_{x}^{2d_{x}\eta}\|f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}-f_{\ast,\mathrm{high}}\ast K_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}^{2}\Big).

The last inequality holds by plugging into the upper bound on
\|T([f_{\lambda}]-f_{\mathrm{aux}})\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}^{2}
and
\|T[f_{\mathrm{aux}}]-Tf_{\ast}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}
derived above.
Notice that the term
\|f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}-f_{\ast,\mathrm{low}}\ast K_{\gamma_{o}}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}
is unnecessarily inflated by the Fourier measure of
partial
ill-posedness
\gamma_{x}^{-d_{x}\eta}\gg 1
(
4.2
) even if there is no smoothing effect of
T
acting on
O
. We obtain

\displaystyle\|[f_{\lambda}]-f_{\ast}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{x}^{-2d_{x}\eta}\left(\lambda\gamma_{x}^{-d_{x}}\gamma_{o}^{-d_{o}}+\gamma_{o}^{2s_{o}}+\gamma_{x}^{2s_{x}+2d_{x}\eta}\right)+\gamma_{o}^{2s_{o}}+\gamma_{x}^{2s_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{-\frac{\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}}{1+2(\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}+\eta)+\frac{d_{o}}{s_{o}}(\frac{s_{x}}{d_{x}}+\eta)}}.

The last step holds by choosing
\lambda,\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}
as in
4.1
. The upper bound on the approximation error does not match the minimax lower bound in
4.2
.

We conclude this section by offering a more practical perspective on why the gap emerges.
Hyperparameters including the kernel lengthscales are selected via cross-validation in practice.
For the KIV-O estimator
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
computed from Stage II samples
\mathcal{D}_{2}=\{(\mathbf{z}_{i},\mathbf{o}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, the kernel lengthscales
\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}
are selected by minimizing the following cross-validation criterion.

\textstyle\mathrm{CV}(\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o},\lambda)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\langle\hat{f}_{-i,\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o},\lambda},\hat{F}_{\xi}(\mathbf{z}_{i},\mathbf{o}_{i})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}})^{2},

(42)

where
\hat{f}_{-i,\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o},\lambda}
denotes the version of
\hat{f}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o},\lambda}
computed from
n-1
samples from
\mathcal{D}_{2}
excluding its
i
-th sample.
This cross-validation criterion
\mathrm{CV}(\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o},\lambda)
is a consistent estimator for the
projected
risk
\|T([\hat{f}_{\lambda}]-f_{*})\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}
and has been used in many 2SLS approaches
(
Hartford et al. 2017
;
Xu et al. 2021b
;
Mastouri et al. 2021
;
Xu and Gretton 2025
)
.

Similarly, the choice of lengthscales
\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o},\lambda
in our upper bound analysis is also obtained by minimizing the projected risk
\|T([\hat{f}_{\lambda}]-f_{*})\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}
which results

\gamma_{x}^{s_{x}+d_{x}\eta}=\gamma_{o}^{s_{o}}
.
This can be contrasted with the choice of kernel lengthscales in anisotropic kernel ridge regression which imposes a different balance condition
\gamma_{x}^{s_{x}}=\gamma_{o}^{s_{o}}

(
Hang and Steinwart 2021
)
. The extra
d_{x}\eta
in the exponent of
\gamma_{x}
arises due to the partial smoothing effect of
T
, which maps a function that is
(s_{x},s_{o})
-smooth on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{O}
to a function that is
(s_{x}+d_{x}\eta,s_{o})
-smooth on
\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}
.
On the other hand, our minimax lower bound requires constructing B-splines in Eq. (
98
) in the Supplement with resolution
\mathfrak{K}
and
J_{x}:=2^{\lfloor\frac{\mathfrak{K}s}{s_{x}}\rfloor}
and
J_{o}:=2^{\lfloor\frac{\mathfrak{K}s}{s_{o}}\rfloor}
.
J_{x},J_{o}
play a role analogous to
\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}
(see
4.1
), but they satisfy the balance condition
J_{x}^{s_{x}}\asymp J_{o}^{s_{o}}
.
This discrepancy between the kernel lengthscales balance condition and B-spline lengthscales balance condition gives rise to the gap between our upper and lower bound.
Simply enforcing
\gamma_{x}^{s_{x}}=\gamma_{o}^{s_{o}}
in our upper bound analysis would result in a slower rate.

6
Conclusion

We study nonparametric instrumental variable regression with observed covariates (NPIV-O), a setting that generalizes NPIV by incorporating observed covariates to enable heterogeneous treatment effect estimation.
The conditional expectation operator
T
behaves as a partial identity operator, which makes NPIV-O a hybrid of NPIV and NPR.
We prove an upper bound for kernel 2SLS and the first minimax lower bound.
Our upper and lower bounds interpolate between the known optimal rates for NPIV and NPR, and adapt to the anisotropic smoothness of
f_{\ast}
.
Our analysis reveals a gap between the upper and lower bounds in the general setting, and closing this gap remains an open direction for NPIV-O.

Appendix A
Examples for the partial smoothing effect of T

In this section, we extend
4.2
and
4.3
from the main text—on the Fourier measure of partial ill-posedness and on the Fourier measure of partial contractivity of the operator
T
, respectively—to the space of distributions. This allows us to apply these assumptions to periodic functions and to construct an explicit example verifying
4.3
. First, we prove the following Lemma relating
4.2
and
4.3
.

Lemma A.1
.

If Assumption
4.2
and
4.3
hold simultaneously, and
P_{XO}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{O}
, then
\eta_{0}\geq\eta_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1)
be arbitrary. Let
\epsilon>0
be arbitrary. We define

\displaystyle f_{\gamma,\epsilon}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{o})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left[\mathbbm{1}[\|\bm{\omega}\|_{2}\in[\gamma^{-1},\gamma^{-1}+\epsilon]]\right](\mathbf{x}).

We have
f_{\gamma,\epsilon}\in\mathrm{LF}\left((\gamma^{-1}+\epsilon)^{-1}\right)\cap\mathrm{HF}(\gamma)\cap L^{\infty}(P_{XO})
. Since
P_{XO}
admits a density function on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{O}
, and
f_{\gamma,\epsilon}
only vanishes on sets of null Lebesgue measure, we have
\|f_{\gamma,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}\neq 0
. As imposed by
4.3
and
4.2
, we thus have

\displaystyle c_{0}^{-1}\left(\gamma^{-1}+\epsilon\right)^{-d_{x}\eta_{0}}\|f_{\gamma,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}\leq\|Tf_{\gamma,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}\leq c_{1}\gamma^{d_{x}\eta_{1}}\|f_{\gamma,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}.

Since
\|f_{\gamma,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}\neq 0
, we have
(\forall\gamma\in(0,1))\;(\forall\epsilon>0)\;c_{0}^{-1}\left(\gamma^{-1}+\epsilon\right)^{-d_{x}\eta_{0}}\leq c_{1}\gamma^{d_{x}\eta_{1}}
. For a fixed
\gamma
, taking the limit
\epsilon\to 0
, we have by continuity

\displaystyle(\forall\gamma\in(0,1))\;\frac{1}{c_{0}c_{1}}\leq\gamma^{d_{x}(\eta_{1}-\eta_{0})}.

(43)

Taking the limit
\gamma\to 1
in Eq. (
43
), we find
c_{0}c_{1}\geq 1
. Then since Eq. (
43
) holds for all
\gamma\in(0,1)
, we find that
\eta_{1}\leq\eta_{0}
.
∎

Definition 5

(Distribution and distribution of a function)
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a domain. A distribution
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
is a continuous linear functional on the space of test functions
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
, where
\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
is the set
C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
endowed with the
canonical limit of Fréchet topology
. For a locally integrable function
f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for all
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
, we define the distribution
T_{f}
by

\displaystyle T_{f}\phi:=\int_{\Omega}f(\mathbf{x})\phi(\mathbf{x})\;\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

Definition 6

(Support of a distribution)
.

A distribution
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Omega)
is supported in the closed set
K\subset\Omega
if
u[\phi]=0

\forall\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega\setminus K)
. The
support
of
u
,
\mathrm{supp}\;u
is the set

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}\;u=\cap\{K:u\text{ is supported in }K\}.

Definition 7

(Tempered distribution)
.

We define the
Schwartz space

\mathcal{S}
via

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\left\{\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mid\forall\bm{\alpha},\forall N\in\mathbb{N},\;\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|(1+|\mathbf{x}|)^{N}D^{\bm{\alpha}}\phi(\mathbf{x})\right|<\infty\right\}.

We say that a sequence
\{\phi_{j}\}^{\infty}_{j=1}\subset\mathcal{S}
tends to zero iff

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|(1+|\mathbf{x}|)^{N}D^{\bm{\alpha}}\phi_{j}(\mathbf{x})|\to 0

for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and all multi-indices
\bm{\alpha}
. This endows
\mathcal{S}
with a topology. We define the space of
tempered distributions
to be the continuous dual space of
\mathcal{S}
, denoted as
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Definition 8

(Periodic distribution)
.

We define the translation of a distribution
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
via
\tau_{\mathbf{z}}u[\phi]=u[\tau_{-\mathbf{z}}\phi]

for all
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where we use
\tau_{\mathbf{z}}\phi(\mathbf{x}):=\phi(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z})
.
We say that a distribution
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is periodic if for each
\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
we have
\tau_{\mathbf{g}}u=u
.
Clearly, if
f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is periodic, then
T_{f}
is a periodic distribution.

Definition 9

(Fourier transform of
L^{1}
functions)
.

For
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we define the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}[f]=\hat{f}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[f](\bm{\xi})=\hat{f}(\bm{\xi}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\mathbf{x})\exp(-i\langle\mathbf{x},\bm{\xi}\rangle)\;\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

Definition 10

(Fourier transform of tempered distributions)
.

For a distribution
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, we define the Fourier transform of
u
, written
\hat{u}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, to be the distribution satisfying:

\displaystyle\hat{u}[\phi]=u[\hat{\phi}],\;\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S},

which is well defined since the Fourier transform maps
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathcal{S}
continuously.

We note that, for all
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
, by the Fourier inversion theorem
[
Rudin 1987
, 9.11]
,

\displaystyle T_{1}[\hat{\phi}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{\phi}(\mathbf{x})\;\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=(2\pi)^{d}\phi(\bm{0})=(2\pi)^{d}\delta_{\bm{0}}[\phi].

Hence,
\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define
e_{\bm{\xi}}(\mathbf{x})=\exp(i2\pi\langle\mathbf{x},\bm{\xi}\rangle)
.
So
\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S}
, we have

\displaystyle T_{e_{\bm{\xi}}}[\hat{\phi}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp(i2\pi\langle\mathbf{x},\bm{\xi}\rangle)\hat{\phi}(\mathbf{x})\;\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=(2\pi)^{d}\phi(2\pi\bm{\xi}).

(44)

i.e.
\mathcal{F}[T_{e_{\bm{\xi}}}]=(2\pi)^{d}\delta_{2\pi\bm{\xi}}
, the Dirac delta distribution at
2\pi\bm{\xi}
. We can now make sense of the Fourier transform of a periodic function. The following Lemma is from
Friedlander 1998
:

Lemma A.2

(Periodic distributions are tempered)
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a periodic distribution. Then
u
is in fact a tempered distribution, i.e.
u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Proposition A.1
.

Suppose
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a periodic distribution. Then there exist constants
c_{\bm{\xi}}\in\mathbb{C}
such that
u
can be represented as a (generalized) Fourier series,

\displaystyle u=\sum_{\bm{\xi}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}c_{\bm{\xi}}\,T_{e_{\bm{\xi}}},

with
c_{\bm{\xi}}
satisfying the bound
|c_{\bm{\xi}}|\leq K(1+|\bm{\xi}|)^{N}
for some
K>0
and
N\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We can now make sense of the following definitions: for any scalar
\gamma\in(0,1)
, we define the following two sets of functions which are generalization of the
\mathrm{LF}(\gamma)
and
\mathrm{HF}(\gamma)
defined in the main text to
distributions
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathrm{LF}(\gamma):=\{f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}+d_{o}})\ |\ &\forall\mathbf{o}\in\mathcal{O},\;T_{f(\cdot,\mathbf{o})}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}}),\\
&\;\mathrm{supp}\big(\mathcal{F}[f(\cdot,\mathbf{o})]\big)\subseteq\left\{\bm{\omega}_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}}:\|\bm{\omega}_{x}\|_{2}\leq\gamma^{-1}\right\}\}.\\
\mathrm{HF}(\gamma):=\{f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}+d_{o}})\ |\ &\forall\mathbf{o}\in\mathcal{O},\;T_{f(\cdot,\mathbf{o})}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}}),\\
&\;\mathrm{supp}\big(\mathcal{F}[f(\cdot,\mathbf{o})]\big)\subseteq\left\{\bm{\omega}_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}}:\|\bm{\omega}_{x}\|_{2}\geq\gamma^{-1}\right\}\}.\end{aligned}

(45)

We now recall the statements of Assumption 4.2 and 4.3 from the main text with the above generalization of
\mathrm{LF}(\gamma)
and
\mathrm{HF}(\gamma)
.

Assumption A.1

(Fourier measure of partial ill-posedness of
T
)
.

There exists a constant
c_{0}>0
and a parameter
\eta_{0}\in[0,\infty)
only depending on
T
, such that for all
\gamma\in(0,1)
and all functions
f\in\mathrm{LF}(\gamma)\cap L^{\infty}(P_{XO})
, the following inequality is satisfied:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}\leq c_{0}\gamma^{-d_{x}\eta_{0}}\|Tf\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}.

In particular,
c_{0}
does not depend on
\gamma
.

Assumption A.2

(Fourier measure of partial contractivity of
T
)
.

There exists a constant
c_{1}>0
and a parameter
\eta_{1}\in[0,\infty)
only depending on
T
, such that for all
\gamma\in(0,1)
and all functions
f\in\mathrm{HF}(\gamma)\cap L^{\infty}(P_{XO})
, the following inequality is satisfied:

\displaystyle\|Tf\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}\leq c_{1}\gamma^{d_{x}\eta_{1}}\|f\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}.

In particular,
c_{1}
does not depend on
\gamma
.

Let
(S^{1},+)
denote the unit circle (equipped with a group structure via addition), and let
\mathrm{d}x
denote the Haar measure on
S^{1}
, which coincides with the pushforward of the Lebesgue measure under the quotient map
S^{1}\cong\frac{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{Z}}
. We make use of the obvious identification between functions on
(S^{1})^{d}
and
1
-periodic functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for any
d\geq 1
.

Definition 11

(Fourier series
[
Katznelson 2004
]
)
.

Let
f\in L^{1}(S^{1})
. We define the
n
th Fourier coefficient of
f
by
\mathcal{F}[f][n]=\int_{S^{1}}f(x)e^{-i2\pi nx}\;\mathrm{d}x
.
The Fourier series of
f\in L^{1}(S^{1})
is the trigonometric series
f(x)=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}[f][n]e^{i2\pi nx}
.

We make use of the group structure of
S^{1}
and the
translation invariance
of the measure
\mathrm{d}x
on
S^{1}
to define the convolution operation in
L^{1}(S^{1})
, following
[
Katznelson 2004
, Section 1.7]
.

Proposition A.2
.

Let
f,g\in L^{1}(S^{1})
. For almost all
t\in S^{1}
, the function
f(t-\tau)g(\tau)
is
L^{1}(S^{1})
-integrable as a function of
\tau
, and if we define the convolution

\displaystyle h(t)=\int_{S^{1}}f(t-\tau)g(\tau)\;\mathrm{d}\tau

then
h\in L^{1}(S^{1})
with
\|h\|_{L^{1}(S^{1})}\leq\|f\|_{L^{1}(S^{1})}\|g\|_{L^{1}(S^{1})}
. Moreover,
(\forall n\in\mathbb{Z})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[h][n]=\mathcal{F}[f][n]\mathcal{F}[g][n].

For any
\gamma>0
, we now exhibit a class of functions
f\in\mathrm{HF}(\gamma)
and a distribution
p(x,z,o)
satisfying the statement in
A.2
, with the help of
A.2
. For simplicity, we assume that
d_{x}=d_{z}=d_{o}=1
. Fix a scalar
\gamma\in(0,1)
. Let
g\in L^{1}\left(S^{1}\right)
be a function whose Fourier coefficients vanish on low frequencies, in the sense that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[g][n]=0\quad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ such that }|n|\leq(2\pi\gamma)^{-1}.

(46)

An example of such a function is
g(x)=\exp(i2\pi mx)
, where
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m>(2\pi\gamma)^{-1}
. Let
h\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{o}})
be such that it does not vanish identically.
We then define
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
:

\displaystyle f(x+t,o):=g(x)h(o)\quad\text{for all }x\in[0,1),t\in\mathbb{Z},o\in\mathbb{R}.

Then it follows that
f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}+d_{o}})
since
g\in L^{1}(S^{1})
and
h\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
. It follows from
A.2
that
(\forall o\in\mathbb{R})
,
T_{f(\cdot,o)}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
for any
o\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, for every
o\in\mathbb{R}
, we observe that: i)
h(o)=0
, then
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{F}_{x}[T_{f(\cdot,o)}])=\emptyset
, ii)
h(o)\neq 0
, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{F}[T_{f(\cdot,o)}])

\displaystyle=\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{F}[T_{g}])

\displaystyle=\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathcal{F}\left[\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}[g][n]\,T_{e^{i2\pi n\cdot}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\mathrm{supp}\left(\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{F}[g][n](2\pi)\delta_{2\pi n}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\mathrm{supp}\left(\sum_{|n|>(2\pi\gamma)^{-1}}\mathcal{F}[g][n](2\pi)\delta_{2\pi n}\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq\left\{\omega_{x}\in\mathbb{R}:|\omega_{x}|>\frac{1}{\gamma}\right\}.

Here, step (a) follows from (
44
) and the use of distributional support as defined in
6
, and step (b) follows from Eq. (
46
). Thus, from both cases, we conclude that
f\in\mathrm{HF}(\gamma)
defined in Eq. (
45
).

Fix an integer
k\geq 1
.
We consider a probability space
\Omega=S^{1}\times S^{1}\times\underbrace{S^{1}\times\dots\times S^{1}}_{k}
, where each copy of
S^{1}
is equipped with its Borel
\sigma
-algebra and the normalized Haar measure, and
\Omega
is equipped with the product Borel
\sigma
-algebra and the product measure. We define the following mappings, where
1\leq i\leq k
:

\displaystyle\pi_{Z}:\Omega\to S^{1},\quad

\displaystyle\pi_{Z}(z,o,u_{1},\dots,u_{k})=z

\displaystyle\pi_{O}:\Omega\to S^{1},\quad

\displaystyle\pi_{O}(z,o,u_{1},\dots,u_{k})=o

\displaystyle\pi_{U_{i}}:\Omega\to S^{1},\quad

\displaystyle\pi_{U_{i}}(z,o,u_{1},\dots,u_{k})=u_{i},

Since
\pi_{Z},\pi_{O},\pi_{U_{i}}
’s are measurable, they are valid random variables, and we also denote them by
Z,O,U_{1},\dots,U_{k}
. We then write
W=0.1(U_{1}+\dots+U_{k})
and
X=Z+W
, where addition is understood as a group operation on
S^{1}
. By construction,
X
is independent of
O
. The random tuple
(X,Z,O)
is as considered in the NPIV-O set-up in the main text.

We are now going to show that there exists some constant
c_{1}>0
(which does not depend on
\gamma
), such that

\displaystyle\|Tf\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}\leq c_{1}\gamma^{k}\|f\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}.

We observe that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|f(x,o)|^{2}p(x,o)\;\mathrm{d}x\;\mathrm{d}o

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{1}\times S^{1}}|f(x,o)|^{2}p(x,o)\;\mathrm{d}x\;\mathrm{d}o=\|g\|_{L^{2}(P_{X})}^{2}\|h\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{O})}.

(47)

We also observe that

\displaystyle\|Tf\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle f,T^{\ast}Tf\rangle_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{1}\times S^{1}}\overline{f(x,o)}(T^{\ast}Tf)(x,o)p(x,o)\;\mathrm{d}x\;\mathrm{d}o.

(48)

We also observe that for all
x\in S^{1}
and
o\in S^{1}
,

\displaystyle(T^{\ast}Tf)(x,o)

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{1}}\int_{S^{1}}f(x^{\prime},o)p(x^{\prime}\mid z,o)\;\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\;p(z\mid x,o)\;\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}\int_{S^{1}}f(x^{\prime},o)\left(\int_{S^{1}}p(x^{\prime}\mid z,o)p(z\mid x,o)\;\mathrm{d}z\right)\;\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{1}}f(x^{\prime},o)L(x,x^{\prime},o)\;\mathrm{d}x^{\prime},

where
(\ast)
follows by Fubini’s theorem.
L
is defined as, for all
x,x^{\prime}\in S^{1}
and
o\in S^{1}
,

\displaystyle L(x,x^{\prime},o):=\int_{S^{1}}p(x^{\prime}\mid z,o)p(z\mid x,o)\;\mathrm{d}z=\int_{S^{1}}p(x^{\prime}\mid z)p(z\mid x)\;\mathrm{d}z.

The last step holds because
X
is independent of
O
.
As
L
is not dependent on
o
, we write
L(x,x^{\prime},o)=L(x,x^{\prime})
. Hence
(\forall x\in S^{1},o\in S^{1})

\displaystyle(T^{\ast}Tf)(x,o)=\left(\int_{S^{1}}g(x^{\prime})L(x,x^{\prime})\;\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\right)h(o).

Hence continuing from Eq. (
48
), we find

\displaystyle\|Tf\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{1}\times S^{1}}\overline{g(x)}\overline{h(o)}(T^{\ast}Tf)(x,o)p(x,o)\;\mathrm{d}x\;\mathrm{d}o

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{S^{1}}\overline{g(x)}\left(\int_{S^{1}}g(x^{\prime})L(x,x^{\prime})p(x)\;\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\right)\;\mathrm{d}x\right)\|h\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{O})}.

(49)

We also notice that
(\forall x\in S^{1},x^{\prime}\in S^{1})
, the following hold via Bayes’ rule and the fact that
p(z)
is the Haar measure on
S^{1}
:

\displaystyle L(x,x^{\prime})p(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{z\in S^{1}}p(x^{\prime}\mid z)p(z\mid x)p(x)\;\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{z\in S^{1}}p(x^{\prime}\mid z)p(x\mid z)p(z)\;\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{z\in S^{1}}p(x^{\prime}\mid z)p(x\mid z)\;\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{z\in S^{1}}p_{W}(x^{\prime}-z)p_{W}(x-z)\;\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{z\in S^{1}}p_{W}(z)p_{W}(z+(x-x^{\prime}))\;\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=:\mathfrak{L}(x-x^{\prime}).

where the second last step follows from the change of variable
z\leftarrow x^{\prime}-z
, and in the last step we use the fact that
L(x,x^{\prime})p(x)
is translation-invariant. As throughout the calculations, the difference
x-x^{\prime}
denotes the group operation on
S^{1}
rather than the usual difference on
\mathbb{R}
. We calculate the Fourier coefficients of
\mathfrak{L}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[\mathfrak{L}][n]

\displaystyle=\int_{w\in S^{1}}\mathfrak{L}(w)e^{-i2\pi nw}\;\mathrm{d}w=\int_{w\in S^{1}}\int_{z\in S^{1}}p_{W}(z)p_{W}(z+w)\;\mathrm{d}z\;e^{-i2\pi nw}\;\mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{z\in S^{1}}p_{W}(z)e^{2\pi inz}\;\mathrm{d}z\right|^{2}=|\mathcal{F}[p_{W}][n]|^{2}.

Since
W=0.1(U_{1}+\dots+U_{k})
, we have
p_{W}
is the
k
-times convolution of the probability density function of
0.1U_{1}
. By the convolution Theorem, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[\mathfrak{L}][n]

\displaystyle=\left|10\int_{0}^{0.1}e^{-2\pi inz}\;\mathrm{d}z\right|^{2k}=\left|\frac{10}{2\pi in}\left(1-e^{-0.2\pi in}\right)\right|^{2k}=\left(\frac{10}{\pi n}\right)^{2k}\sin^{2k}(0.1\pi n).

(50)

Hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[\mathfrak{L}][n]\leq\left(\frac{10}{\pi n}\right)^{2k}.

(51)

We have

\displaystyle\frac{\|Tf\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}}{\|h\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{O})}}

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{1}}\overline{g(x)}\left(\int_{S^{1}}g(x^{\prime})\mathfrak{L}(x-x^{\prime})\;\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\right)\;\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\overline{\mathcal{F}[g][n]}\mathcal{F}\left[\int_{S^{1}}g(x^{\prime})\mathfrak{L}(\cdot-x^{\prime})\;\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\right][n]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\overline{\mathcal{F}[g][n]}\mathcal{F}[g][n]\mathcal{F}[\mathfrak{L}][n]=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}|\mathcal{F}[g][n]|^{2}\cdot\mathcal{F}[\mathfrak{L}][n]

(52)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z},|n|>(2\pi\gamma)^{-1}}|\mathcal{F}[g][n]|^{2}\cdot\mathcal{F}[\mathfrak{L}][n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\leq}}(20\gamma)^{2k}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z},|n|>(2\pi\gamma)^{-1}}|\mathcal{F}[g][n]|^{2}

\displaystyle=(20\gamma)^{2k}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}|\mathcal{F}[g][n]|^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}(20\gamma)^{2k}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(S^{1})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f)}}{{=}}(20\gamma)^{2k}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}.

In the above derivations, step
(a)
follows from Parseval’s theorem
[
Katznelson 2004
, 5.4]
and the fact that
\{e^{i2\pi nx}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
forms an orthonormal system in
L^{2}(S^{1})
, step
(b)
follows from
A.2
, step
(c)
follows Eq. (
46
), step
(d)
follows from Eq. (
51
), step
(e)
follows again from Parseval’s Theorem, and finally step
(f)
follows from the fact that
P_{X}
is the Haar measure on
S^{1}
. Continuing from Eq. (
49
), we thus have

\displaystyle\|Tf\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}\leq(20\gamma)^{2k}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}\|h\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{O})}=(20\gamma)^{2k}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{XO})},

where the last equality follows from Eq. (
A
). Therefore, we have proved that
A.2
is satisfied with
\eta_{1}=k
and
c_{1}=20^{k}
. For any
\gamma>0
, we now exhibit a class of functions
f^{\prime}\in\mathrm{LF}(\gamma)
and a distribution
p(x,z,o)
satisfying the statement in
A.1
. We let
p(x,z,o)
be the probability distribution constructed above. Let
g^{\prime}\in L^{1}(S^{1})
be a function whose Fourier coefficients vanish on high frequencies, in the sense that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[g^{\prime}][n]=0\quad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ such that }|n|\geq(2\pi\gamma)^{-1}.

We further assume that
\mathcal{F}[g^{\prime}][n]\neq 0
only if
\sin^{2k}(0.1\pi n)\geq c
for a fixed positive constant
c>0
. Let
h\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{o}})
be such that it does not vanish identically. We then define
f^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
:

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(x+t,o):=g^{\prime}(x)h(o)\quad\text{for all }x\in[0,1),t\in\mathbb{Z},o\in\mathbb{R}.

We can show that
f^{\prime}\in\mathrm{LF}(\gamma)
defined in Eq. (
45
) via a similar argument as before. By Eq. (
52
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\|Tf^{\prime}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}}{\|h\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{O})}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}|\mathcal{F}[g^{\prime}][n]|^{2}\cdot\mathcal{F}[\mathfrak{L}][n]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z},|n|<(2\pi\gamma)^{-1}}|\mathcal{F}[g^{\prime}][n]|^{2}\cdot\mathcal{F}[\mathfrak{L}][n]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z},|n|<(2\pi\gamma)^{-1}}|\mathcal{F}[g^{\prime}][n]|^{2}\left(\frac{10}{\pi n}\right)^{2k}\sin^{2k}(0.1\pi n)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z},|n|<(2\pi\gamma)^{-1}}|\mathcal{F}[g^{\prime}][n]|^{2}\left(\frac{10}{\pi n}\right)^{2k}c

\displaystyle\geq c(20\gamma)^{2k}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z},|n|<(2\pi\gamma)^{-1}}|\mathcal{F}[g^{\prime}][n]|^{2}=c(20\gamma)^{2k}\|g^{\prime}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(S^{1})},

where step
(a)
follows from Eq. (
50
), and step
(b)
follows from the assumption on the Fourier coefficient of
g
. We thus have

\displaystyle\|Tf^{\prime}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}\geq c(20\gamma)^{2k}\|f^{\prime}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}.

Appendix B
Explicit Solutions of KIV-O

The following derivation is adapted from
Meunier et al. 2024a
, which only covers the case of no observed covariates. We refer the reader to
Singh et al. 2019
for the original derivation of closed-form solution for KIV with no observed covariates, and to
Mastouri et al. 2021
,
Singh 2020
,
Xu and Gretton 2025
for derivation of closed-form solutions for the RKHS two-stage proximal causal learning framework, which is mathematically equivalent to KIV with observed covariates, as discussed in
Section

3.1
. Whenever an operator or Gram matrix require distinguishing between Stage I or Stage II kernel on
O
, we denote this via
;1
or
;2
in the subscript.
\odot
denotes Hadamard product. For a matrix
J\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
,
J_{\cdot,j}
denotes its
j
th column.

Stage 1
We follow the closed-form solution given in
Li et al. 2022
. We define

\displaystyle\bm{\Phi}_{\tilde{Z}\tilde{O};1}:\mathcal{H}_{ZO}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{n}},

\displaystyle\quad\bm{\Phi}_{\tilde{Z}\tilde{O};1}=\left[\phi_{Z}\left(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{1}\right)\otimes\phi_{O,1}\left(\tilde{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\right),\ldots,\phi_{Z}\left(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{\tilde{n}}\right)\otimes\phi_{O,1}(\tilde{\mathbf{o}}_{\tilde{n}})\right]^{*},

\displaystyle\bm{\Phi}_{\tilde{X}}:\mathcal{H}_{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{n}},

\displaystyle\quad\bm{\Phi}_{\tilde{X}}=\left[\phi_{X}\left(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\right),\ldots,\phi_{X}\left(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{\tilde{n}}\right)\right]^{*}

We obtain the following estimator

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{\xi}(\cdot,\cdot)=\hat{C}_{X\mid Z,O;\xi}\phi_{Z}(\cdot)\otimes\phi_{O,1}(\cdot),\quad\hat{C}_{X\mid Z,O;\xi}=\bm{\Phi}_{\tilde{X}}^{\ast}\left(K_{\tilde{Z}\tilde{O};1}+\tilde{n}\xi\mathrm{Id}\right)^{-1}\bm{\Phi}_{\tilde{Z}\tilde{O};1},

(53)

where we introduce the Gram matrix

\displaystyle K_{\tilde{Z}\tilde{O};1}=\bm{\Phi}_{\tilde{Z}\tilde{O};1}\bm{\Phi}_{\tilde{Z}\tilde{O};1}^{\ast},\quad[K_{\tilde{Z}\tilde{O};1}]_{ij}=k_{Z}(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{i},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{j})k_{O,1}(\tilde{\mathbf{o}}_{i},\tilde{\mathbf{o}}_{j}).

Stage 2
The Stage 2 solution can be written as

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\lambda}=\left(\frac{1}{n}\bm{\Phi}_{\hat{F}O;2}^{\ast}\bm{\Phi}_{\hat{F}O;2}+\lambda\mathrm{Id}\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{n}\bm{\Phi}_{\hat{F}O;2}^{\ast}\mathbf{Y}

where we define

\displaystyle\bm{\Phi}_{\hat{F}O;2}:\mathcal{H}_{XO}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\quad\bm{\Phi}_{\hat{F}O;2}=\left[\hat{F}_{\xi}(\mathbf{z}_{1},\mathbf{o}_{1})\otimes\phi_{O,2}(\mathbf{o}_{1}),\dots,\hat{F}_{\xi}(\mathbf{z}_{n},\mathbf{o}_{n})\otimes\phi_{O,2}(\mathbf{o}_{n})\right]^{\ast}.

We then write this in a dual form

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\lambda}=\bm{\Phi}_{\hat{F}O;2}^{\ast}\left(K_{\hat{F}O;2}+n\lambda\mathrm{Id}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{Y}.

where we introduce the Gram matrix

\displaystyle K_{\hat{F}O;2}=\bm{\Phi}_{\hat{F}O;2}\bm{\Phi}_{\hat{F}O;2}^{\ast},\quad[K_{\hat{F}O;2}]_{ij}=\langle\hat{F}_{\xi}(\mathbf{z}_{i},\mathbf{o}_{i}),\hat{F}_{\xi}(\mathbf{z}_{j},\mathbf{o}_{j})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{X}}k_{O,2}(\mathbf{o}_{i},\mathbf{o}_{j}).

By Eq. (
53
), we obtain, for
1\leq j\leq n
,

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{\xi}(\mathbf{z}_{j},\mathbf{o}_{j})=\bm{\Phi}_{\tilde{X}}^{\ast}\underbrace{\left(K_{\tilde{Z}\tilde{O},1}+\tilde{n}\xi\mathrm{Id}\right)^{-1}\left(K_{\tilde{Z}Z}\odot K_{\tilde{O}O;1}\right)_{:j}}_{=:J_{:j}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{n}}J_{ij}\phi_{X}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i}),

(54)

where
J
as defined column-wise is a
\tilde{n}\times n
matrix, and we define the (cross) Gram matrices

\displaystyle[K_{\tilde{Z}Z}]_{ij}=k_{Z}(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{j}),\quad[K_{\tilde{O}O;1}]_{ij}=k_{O,1}(\tilde{\mathbf{o}}_{i},\mathbf{o}_{j}),

for
1\leq i\leq\tilde{n}
,
1\leq j\leq n
. Consequently, for
1\leq i,j\leq n
, we have

\displaystyle K_{\hat{F}O;2}=\left(J^{T}K_{\tilde{X}\tilde{X}}J\right)\odot K_{OO;2},

where we define the Gram matrices

\displaystyle[K_{\tilde{X}\tilde{X}}]_{ij}=k_{X}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{j}),\quad[K_{OO;2}]_{lm}=k_{O,2}(\mathbf{o}_{l},\mathbf{o}_{m}),

for
1\leq i,j\leq\tilde{n}
,
1\leq l,m\leq n
. For a new test point
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{o})\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{O}
, we have, for
1\leq j\leq n
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\phi_{X}(\mathbf{x})\otimes\phi_{O,2}(\mathbf{o}),\hat{F}_{\xi}(\mathbf{z}_{j},\mathbf{o}_{j})\otimes\phi_{O,2}(\mathbf{o}_{j})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{XO}}

\displaystyle=k_{O,2}(\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}_{j})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{n}}J_{ij}k_{X}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\mathbf{x})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(K_{O\mathbf{o},2}\odot(J^{T}K_{\tilde{X}\mathbf{x}})\right)_{j},

where we define
K_{\tilde{X}\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{n}\times 1}
and
K_{O\mathbf{o},2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 1}
respectively as follows:

\displaystyle[K_{\tilde{X}\mathbf{x}}]_{i}=k_{X}(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\mathbf{x}),\quad[K_{O\mathbf{o},2}]_{i}=k_{O,2}(\mathbf{o}_{i},\mathbf{o}).

Thus we have

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{o})

\displaystyle=\left\langle\phi_{X}(\mathbf{x})\otimes\phi_{O,2}(\mathbf{o}),\bm{\Phi}_{\hat{F}O;2}^{\ast}\left(K_{\hat{F}O;2}+n\lambda\mathrm{Id}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{Y}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{XO}}

\displaystyle=\left(K_{O\mathbf{o},2}^{T}\odot(K_{\tilde{X}\mathbf{x}}^{T}J)\right)\left(\left(J^{T}K_{\tilde{X}\tilde{X}}J\right)\odot K_{OO;2}+n\lambda\mathrm{Id}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{Y},

where the last line follows by Eq. (
54
),
Thus the derivation is concluded.

Appendix C
RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{FO}

We recall that the NPIV-O problem can be written as

\displaystyle Y=(Tf_{\ast})(Z,O)+\upsilon,\quad\E[\upsilon\mid Z,O]=0,

(55)

where
\upsilon:=f_{\ast}(X,O)-(Tf_{\ast})(Z,O)+\epsilon
. As introduced in
Meunier et al. 2024a
, we define an RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
induced by the statistical inverse problem Eq. (
55
), following
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
. Our construction can be obtained from that of
Meunier et al. 2024a
with an appropriate feature map construction, as follows. Recall the definition of
F_{\ast}:\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{H}_{X,\gamma_{x}}
that for any
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{Z},\mathbf{o}\in\mathcal{O}
,
F_{\ast}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})=\mathbb{E}[\phi_{X,\gamma_{x}}(X)\mid Z=\mathbf{z},O=\mathbf{o}]
.

Definition 12
.

We define a reproducing kernel Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{FO}=\left\{f:\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}\mid\exists w\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}},\;f(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\equiv\langle w,F_{\ast}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}\right\},

equipped with the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{FO}}:=\inf\left\{\|w\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}\mid f(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\equiv\langle w,F_{\ast}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}\right\}.

We observe that
(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\mapsto F_{*}\left(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o}\right)\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o})
is
not
the canonical feature map of
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
. To construct its canonical feature map, we define
V:\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}\to\mathcal{H}_{FO}
such that

(Vw)(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\equiv\langle w,F_{\ast}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}},\quad w\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}.

From Theorem 4.21 of
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
,
V
is a metric surjection.
By definition, for any
f\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}
, we have

\displaystyle(Vf)(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})=\langle f,F_{\ast}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}=\E[f(X,O)\mid Z=\mathbf{z},O=\mathbf{o}]=(T[f])(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o}).

Furthermore, we know that
V
is also a surjective partial isometry, i.e.
V
is surjective and satisfies

\displaystyle(\forall f\in\ker(V)^{\perp}),\quad\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}=\|Vf\|_{\mathcal{H}_{FO}}.

Equivalently,
(\forall r\in\mathcal{H}_{FO})
,

\displaystyle\|r\|_{\mathcal{H}_{FO}}=\inf\{\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}:h\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}},\;r=Vh\},

(56)

Thus, the canonical feature map of
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
is
(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\mapsto V(F_{\ast}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o}))
, a fact which was also observed on
Meunier et al. 2024a
.

Recall the definition of
f_{\lambda}
in Eq. (
25
),

\displaystyle f_{\lambda}:=\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}\lambda\|f\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}+\|T([f]-f_{\ast})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}.

(57)

and
\bar{f}_{\lambda}
in Eq. (
23
),

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{\lambda}:=\argmin_{f\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}\lambda\|f\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\langle f,F_{\ast}(\mathbf{z}_{i},\mathbf{o}_{i})\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o}_{i})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}})^{2}.

(58)

We define the images of
f_{\lambda}
and
\bar{f}_{\lambda}
respectively under the metric surjection
V
as follows:

\displaystyle h_{\lambda}:=Vf_{\lambda},\quad\bar{h}_{\lambda}:=V\bar{f}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{H}_{FO},\quad h_{\ast}:=Tf_{\ast}.

We observe by combining Eq. (
56
) and Eq. (
58
) that
\overline{h}_{\lambda}
is the solution to a standard kernel ridge regression problem with the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
:

\displaystyle\bar{h}_{\lambda}=\argmin_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{FO}}\lambda\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}_{FO}}^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(h(\mathbf{z}_{i},\mathbf{o}_{i})-y_{i})^{2}.

(59)

Similarly, for
h_{\lambda}
we have

\displaystyle h_{\lambda}=\argmin_{h\in\mathcal{H}_{FO}}\lambda\|h\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}_{FO}}+\|h_{\ast}-h\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}.

(60)

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\left\|T\left(\left[\bar{f}_{\lambda}\right]-[f_{\lambda}]\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}=\left\|\left[V\bar{f}_{\lambda}\right]-[Vf_{\lambda}]\right\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}=\left\|\left[\bar{h}_{\lambda}\right]-\left[h_{\lambda}\right]\right\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})},

(61)

which means that the projected error
\|T([\bar{f}_{\lambda}]-[f_{\lambda}]])\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}
has been translated to the generalization error
\left\|\left[\bar{h}_{\lambda}\right]-[h_{\lambda}]\right\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}
of a standard kernel ridge regression (KRR) with this new hypothesis space
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
, which allows us to apply techniques from the analysis of kernel ridge regression. To this end, we will analyse the capacity (
Section

C.1
) along with the embedding property (
Section

C.2
) of
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
, both of which are crucial properties for characterizing the generalization error of KRR
[
Fischer and Steinwart 2020
]
.

C.1
Capacity of
\mathcal{H}_{FO}

We have

\displaystyle\quad\sup_{\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}^{\prime}}\sup_{\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}\left\langle V(F_{\ast}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o})),V(F_{\ast}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime})\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o}^{\prime}))\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{FO}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}^{\prime}}\sup_{\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}\left\langle F_{\ast}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o}),F_{\ast}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime})\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o}^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}^{\prime}}k_{O,\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}^{\prime})\sup_{\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}k_{X,\gamma_{x}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})p(\mathbf{x}\mid\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})p(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mid\mathbf{z}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime})\;\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\;\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}^{\prime}}k_{O,\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}^{\prime})\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}k_{X,\gamma_{x}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\sup_{\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}\iint_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}}p(\mathbf{x}\mid\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})p(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\mid\mathbf{z}^{\prime},\mathbf{o}^{\prime})\;\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\;\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}^{\prime}}k_{O,\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o},\mathbf{o}^{\prime})\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}k_{X,\gamma_{x}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}).

Hence the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
has a bounded kernel, therefore by
Steinwart and Scovel 2012
,
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
is compactly embedded into
L^{2}(P_{ZO})
. We define the covariance operator
C_{FO}:\mathcal{H}_{FO}\to\mathcal{H}_{FO}

\displaystyle C_{FO}:=V\mathbb{E}_{ZO}\left[(F_{\ast}(Z,O)\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(O))\otimes(F_{\ast}(Z,O)\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(O))\right]V^{\ast}

It is a self-adjoint compact operator by
Steinwart and Scovel 2012
.
The spectral theorem for self-adjoint compact
operators
[
Reed and Simon 1980
, Theorems VI.16,
VI.17]
yields, there exists countable
\mu_{FO,1}\geq\mu_{FO,2}\geq\dots\geq 0
,
([e_{FO,i}])_{i\geq 1}
an orthonormal system of
L^{2}(P_{ZO})
and
\left(\sqrt{\mu_{FO,i}}e_{FO,i}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{FO}
an orthonormal system in
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
, such that

\displaystyle C_{FO}=\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{FO,i}\langle\cdot,\sqrt{\mu_{FO,i}}e_{FO,i}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{FO}}\sqrt{\mu_{FO,i}}e_{FO,i}.

(62)

Definition 13

(Effective dimension)
.

The effective dimension of
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
, denoted as
\mathcal{N}_{FO}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{FO}(\lambda)=\mathrm{tr}\left((C_{FO}+\lambda)^{-1}C_{FO}\right)=\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{\mu_{FO,i}}{\mu_{FO,i}+\lambda}.

Proposition C.1
.

Let
n\geq 10
and
\lambda=n^{-1}
. Let
\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}\in(0,1]
be the lengthscales for the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}
. Suppose that the distribution
P_{XO}
satisfies Assumption
2.2
. Then we have,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{FO}(\lambda)\leq C^{\prime}(\log n)^{d_{x}+d_{o}+1}\left(\gamma_{x}^{d_{x}}\gamma_{o}^{d_{o}}\right)^{-1}

for some constant
C^{\prime}
independent of
n,\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}
.

Proof.

From
C.1
and
C.1
and setting
p=\frac{1}{\log n}
,
(\forall i\geq 1)
, we have

\displaystyle\mu_{FO,i}\leq(C\log n)^{2(d_{x}+d_{o}+1)\log n}(\gamma_{x}^{d_{x}}\gamma_{o}^{d_{o}})^{-2\log n}i^{-2\log n}.

Next, we use
[
Caponnetto and De Vito 2007
, Proposition 3]
(with error corrected in
Sutherland 2017
) to obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{FO}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\pi/(2\log n)}{\sin(\pi/(2\log n))}\left((C\log n)^{2(d_{x}+d_{o}+1)\log n}(\gamma_{x}^{d_{x}}\gamma_{o}^{d_{o}})^{-2\log n}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\log n}}\lambda^{-(2\log n)^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi/(2\log n)}{\sin(\pi/(2\log n))}(C\log n)^{(d_{x}+d_{o}+1)}\left(\gamma_{x}^{d_{x}}\gamma_{o}^{d_{o}}\right)^{-1}\lambda^{-(2\log n)^{-1}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}3C^{(d_{x}+d_{o}+1)}(\log n)^{(d_{x}+d_{o}+1)}\left(\gamma_{x}^{d_{x}}\gamma_{o}^{d_{o}}\right)^{-1}n^{(2\log n)^{-1}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}3\sqrt{e}C^{(d_{x}+d_{o}+1)}(\log n)^{(d_{x}+d_{o}+1)}\left(\gamma_{x}^{d_{x}}\gamma_{o}^{d_{o}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=C^{\prime}(\log n)^{d_{x}+d_{o}+1}\left(\gamma_{x}^{d_{x}}\gamma_{o}^{d_{o}}\right)^{-1},

where
C^{\prime}=\sqrt{e}3C^{(d_{x}+d_{o}+1)}
.
In the above chain of derivations,
(i)
holds because
\frac{t}{\sin t}\leq 3
for
t\leq\pi/2
and
\pi/(2\log n)\leq\pi/2
and
(ii)
holds because
n^{\frac{1}{2\log n}}=\sqrt{e}
for
n>1
.
∎

In the proof of
C.1
, to bound the effective dimension, we need to bound the eigendecay of the compact self-adjoint operator
C_{FO}
. We first control the decay of the entropy numbers of the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
, which translates into a bound on the eigendecay of
C_{FO}
as shown in
C.1
. In
C.2
, we show that the
i
th entropy number of
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
is bounded above by the
i
th entropy number of
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}
. The entropy numbers of
\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}
are well-understood by the results of
Hang and Steinwart 2021
(restated in
C.1
), which completes the derivation.

In this section, for real-valued Hilbert spaces
E,F
and a bounded, linear, compact operator
S:E\to F
,
s_{i}(S)
denotes the
i
th singular value of
S
, as defined in
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
;
e_{i}(S)
denotes the
i
th entropy number of
S
, as defined in
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
;
a_{i}(S)
denotes the
i
th approximation number of
S
, as defined in
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
.

Lemma C.1
.

Suppose that
P_{XO}
satisfies
2.2
in the main text. Then,
(\forall i\geq 1)
,
(\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}\in(0,1])
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any
p>0
,

\displaystyle e_{i}(\mathrm{id}:\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(P_{XO}))\leq(3C)^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\frac{d_{x}+d_{o}+1}{ep}\right)^{\frac{d_{x}+d_{o}+1}{p}}\left(\gamma_{x}^{d_{x}}\gamma_{o}^{d_{o}}\right)^{-\frac{1}{p}}i^{-\frac{1}{p}}.

Proof.

The corollary follows immediately from
Hang and Steinwart 2021
by setting
\bm{\gamma}=[\underbrace{\gamma_{x},\ldots,\gamma_{x}}_{d_{x}},\underbrace{\gamma_{o},\ldots,\gamma_{o}}_{d_{o}}]^{\top}\in(0,1]^{d_{x}+d_{o}}
, and noting that
L^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{O})
continuously embeds into
L^{2}(P_{XO})
with
\|L^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{O})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(P_{XO})\|\leq 1
and
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
.
∎

Lemma C.2
.

We have,
(\forall i\geq 1)
,

\displaystyle e_{i}(\mathrm{id}:\mathcal{H}_{FO}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(P_{ZO}))\leq e_{i}(\mathrm{id}:\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(P_{XO})).

Proof.

Fix
i\geq 1
. For a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
,
B_{\mathcal{H}}
denotes the unit ball in
\mathcal{H}
, and
B(x,r,\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}})
denotes the ball in
\mathcal{H}
centred at
x\in\mathcal{H}
with radius
r
. Fix
\epsilon>0
such that
\exists g_{1},\dots,g_{2^{i-1}}\in B_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}
such that

\displaystyle B_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{2^{i-1}}B\left(g_{i},\epsilon,\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}\right).

Fix
f\in B_{\mathcal{H}_{FO}}
and an arbitrary
\tilde{\epsilon}>0
. By
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
, there exists
g\in\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}
such that

\displaystyle f=\langle g,F_{\ast}(\cdot,\cdot)\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\cdot)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}=Vg,

and
\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}\leq 1+\tilde{\epsilon}
. By the preceding statement,
(\exists i\in\{1,\dots,2^{i-1}\})
such that

\displaystyle\|g-(1+\tilde{\epsilon})g_{i}\|_{L^{2}(P_{XO})}\leq\epsilon(1+\tilde{\epsilon}).

For
i\in\{1,\dots,2^{i-1}\}
, define
f_{i}=(1+\tilde{\epsilon})Vg_{i}=(1+\tilde{\epsilon})\langle g_{i},F_{\ast}(\cdot,\cdot)\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\cdot)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\|f-f_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}}\langle g-(1+\tilde{\epsilon})g_{i},F_{\ast}(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\otimes\phi_{\gamma_{o}}(\mathbf{o})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}}^{2}p(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\;\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}\;\mathrm{d}\mathbf{o}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}}\mathbb{E}[(g-(1+\tilde{\epsilon})g_{i})(X,O)\mid Z=\mathbf{z},O=\mathbf{o}]^{2}p(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{o})\;\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}\;\mathrm{d}\mathbf{o}

\displaystyle\leq\|g-(1+\tilde{\epsilon})g_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{XO})},

where the inequality is deduced by Jensen’s inequality. We find thus that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{B_{FO}}\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{2^{i-1}}B(f_{i},\epsilon(1+\tilde{\epsilon}),\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}).

Hence
e_{i}(\mathrm{id}:\mathcal{H}_{FO}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(P_{ZO}))\leq\epsilon(1+\tilde{\epsilon})
. Since
\epsilon>e_{i}(\mathrm{id}:\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(P_{XO}))
and
\tilde{\epsilon}>0
are arbitrary, it follows that
e_{i}(\mathrm{id}:\mathcal{H}_{FO}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(P_{ZO}))\leq e_{i}(\mathrm{id}:\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(P_{XO}))
.
∎

Corollary C.1
.

Suppose that
P_{XO}
satisfies
2.2
in the main text. We have that

\displaystyle\mu_{FO,i}\leq 4e_{i}(\mathrm{id}:\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(P_{XO}))^{2}

Proof.

Let
\iota_{FO}:\mathcal{H}_{FO}\to L^{2}(P_{ZO})
denote the embedding
\mathcal{H}_{FO}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(P_{ZO})
. We have,
(\forall i\geq 1)
, the following chain of derivations

\displaystyle\mu_{FO,i}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{i}(\iota_{FO}^{\ast}\iota_{FO})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}s_{i}(\iota_{FO})^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}a_{i}(\iota_{FO})^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}4e_{i}(\iota_{FO})^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\leq}}4e_{i}(\mathrm{id}:\mathcal{H}_{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{o}}\hookrightarrow L^{2}(P_{XO}))^{2}.

In the above derivations,
(a)
follows from
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
,
(b)
follows from the paragraph after
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
,
(c)
follows from
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
, and
(d)
follows from
C.2
.
∎

C.2
Embedding property of
\mathcal{H}_{FO}

Definition 14

(Continuous embedding)
.

A Hilbert space
(X,\|\cdot\|)
is said to continuously embed into Hilbert space
(Y,\|\cdot\|)
if
X\subset Y
and there exists a constant
C
such that
\|x\|_{Y}\leq C\|x\|_{X}
for all
x\in X
.
We denote this as
X\hookrightarrow Y
. The embedding norm
\|X\hookrightarrow Y\|
is defined as the smallest constant
C
for which the above inequality holds.

Definition 15

(Interpolation space)
.

Assume that
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are Hilbert spaces and
X_{1}\subseteq X_{0}
. For
\theta\in(0,1)
and
x\in X_{0}
, we define the
K
-functional

K(t,x;X_{0},X_{1})
as follows

\displaystyle K(t,x;X_{0},X_{1}):=\inf_{y\in X_{1}}\left\{\|x-y\|_{X_{0}}+t\|y\|_{X_{1}}\right\}.

(63)

For
\theta\in(0,1)
, we define
interpolation space

Hytönen et al. 2016
[Definition C.3.1]

\displaystyle(X_{0},X_{1})_{\theta,2}:=\{x\in X_{0}\mid\|x\|_{\theta,2}<\infty\},\quad\|x\|_{\theta,2}:=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(t^{-\theta}K(t,x;X_{0},X_{1})\right)^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We have proved in
Section

C.1
that
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
can be compactly embedded into
L^{2}(P_{ZO})
.
Since
P_{ZO}
is equivalent to the Lebesgue measure over
\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O}
as per
4.4
,
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
can also be compactly embedded into
L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O})
.
So we can define the
\theta
-power space of
\mathcal{H}_{FO}
following
[
Steinwart and Scovel 2012
, Eq. (36)]

\left([\mathcal{H}_{FO}]^{\theta}_{P_{ZO}},\|\cdot\|_{[\mathcal{H}_{FO}]^{\theta}_{P_{ZO}}}\right):=\left\{\sum_{i\geq 1}a_{i}\mu_{FO,i}^{\theta/2}[e_{FO,i}]:\;(a_{i})\in\ell_{2}(\mathbb{N})\right\}\subseteq L^{2}(P_{ZO}).

For
0<\theta<1
, the
\theta
-power space coincides with the interpolation space
[\mathcal{H}_{FO}]^{\theta}_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}\cong[L_{2}(P_{ZO}),[\mathcal{H}_{FO}]_{P_{ZO}}]_{\theta,2}

[
Steinwart and Scovel 2012
, Theorem 4.6]
. We write
[\mathcal{H}_{FO}]_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}:=[\mathcal{H}_{FO}]_{L^{2}(P_{ZO})}^{1}
. Similarly, we write
[\mathcal{H}_{FO}]_{L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O})}:=[\mathcal{H}_{FO}]_{L^{2}(\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{O})}^{1}
.

Proposition C.2
.

Suppose Assumption
2.2
holds, and let
m_{o},m_{z},\rho
be as defined in the statement of Assumption
2.2
. Suppose
\theta\in(0,1)
satisfies
\frac{d_{o}}{2m_{o}\theta}<1
and
\frac{d_{z}}{2m_{z}\theta}<1
. Then there exists a constant
C_{\theta}>0
independent of
n
such that

\displaystyle\left\|k_{FO}^{\theta}\right\|_{\infty}:=\left\|[\mathcal{H}_{FO}]^{\theta}_{P_{ZO}}\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(P_{ZO})\right\|\lesssim C_{\theta}\rho^{\theta}\gamma_{o}^{-\theta m_{o}}.

Proof.

By
C.3
, we have
